Chapter 0 : Executive Summary 
 
 
0.1  INTRODUCTION  
 
Gujarat Metro Rail Corporation Ltd. intends to develop the Surat Metro Rail Phase -I from 
Sarthana to Dream City (21.61 Km) and Bhesan to Saroli (18.74 km).  
 
0.1.1  Objective of the Study  
 
The objective of the present study is to carry out Envir onmental Impact Assessment (EIA) 
and preparation of Environmental Management Plan (EMP) for both the corridors.  
 
0.1.2  Scope of EIA Study  
 
The scope of work for the EIA study was issued vides LOA No. GMRC/EIA & SIA/ Surat/PH-
1/2019/LOA dated 20-03-2019. The scope of Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) 
comprises environmental baseline, impacts resulting from pre -construction, construction 
and operation phases and measures to mitigate adverse impacts due to the project on the 
environment.  
 
0.2  LEGAL, POLICY AND INSTITUTIONAL REQUIREMENTS  
 
The Indian Acts, Rules and Notifications applicable to the proposed project have been 
considered while p reparing the EIA report. In addition to these, requirement as per AFD, 
KfW and World Bank’s Environment and Social Framework 2017 have also been reffered. As 
per the MoEFCC, GoI Notification metro rail project does not require prior environmental 
clearance. Gap Analysis between worldbank guidelines, IFC and GOI regulations are briefed 
in Chapter 2. 
 
0.3  PROJECT DESCRIPTION  
 
Surat metro Phase I  project ha ving two corridors has a total length of 40.35 km with 38 
stations. Sartthana as elevated section and heads towards Dream City as UG section  of 
6.47km. Total 20 stations have been proposed along the corridor  1 of which 14 stations are 
elevated and 6 stations are UG. Second corridor starts from Bhesan and heads towards 
Saroli, this  section is fully elevated. Total 18 stations have been proposed along this 
corridor. Alignments of proposed Surat Metro Rail Corridors are showin in Figure 0.1.  
 
0.3.1  Analysis of Alternatives  
 
A Comprehensive Mobility Plan (CMP) aims to retain the compact structure of the city of 
Surat and proposes intensification of development along the rapid transit corridors. The 
report recommends the  3 corridors as potential metro network as 1. Dream city – Kamrej 
(28.9 km), 2. Bhesan – Umbhel (26.3 km) and 3. Majuragate to Karamala (15.8 km). Study 
was done on the basis of demand analysis and network characteristics, in order to assess 
suitability of a rapid transit mode for these three corridors.  

[page 15]
EIA Study of Surat Metro Rail Project Phase-I
Revised Draft EIA Report July 2020 0.2
Figure 0.1: Surat Metro Rail Corridors


[page 16]
EIA and SIA Study for Surat Metro Rail Project Phase-I 
EIA Report December 2020 3 
 
Economic analysis have been undertaken to assess economic effects of alternative transit 
system options for identified corridors.  Alignments selected on the consi derations of direct 
and indirect benefits with metro like time save, pollution reduction, reductions in road 
accidents etc. During the DPR preparation the section finalized based on various design 
considerations and alternative construction techniques to r educe the impact on 
environmental and social aspects. 
 
0.4  ENVIRONMENTAL BASELINE DATA 
 
The Environmental baseline data collected for the study includes secondary data and 
primary data on quality of air, water and soil; noise; vibration; flora and fauna a nd sensitive 
receptors along the corridors.  
 
0.4.1  Land Environment  
 
Physiography: Surat is a port city situated on the banks of the Tapti river which was formally 
known as Tapi river. The city is located at  21°10′N and 72°50′E. It has an average elevation 
of 13  metres. The project area is with elevation range of 0m to 30m. However as per the 
current ground levels, the pr oposed metro alignment is passing through 6m to 18m 
elevations. 
 
Geology and Hydrogeology : Major geological formations exposed in the district are 
Quaternary alluvium, Tertiary limestone and sandstones and Deccan Trap basalt. Surat 
comes under cenozoic era of tertiary period of Palaeocene series/epoch. The major aquifers 
in the district are fo rmed by alluvium and Deccan Trap basalt with Tertiary formations 
occupying a small patch. The alluvium occurs in the western part of the district and along 
the streams . In Alluvial areas the depth of the tube wells range from 36 m to 169m with 
piezometric levels varying between 2m to 10m. 
 
Soils: In coastal region the soil is deep to very deep dark grey to black colour. The texture is 
clay loam to silty loam. Surat having costal alluvium and costal alluvium (saline) soils. To get 
the actual charectaristics of soil in the project area, soil samples from various locations were 
collected and analyzed to understand the soil characteristics of the area with respect to its 
productivity potential. Ten representative soil samples have been collected along the 
proposed metro alignment during 29/5/2019 to 7/6/2019.   
 
Land Use Pattern:  The land use of 15 km belt on either side of the project corridors has 
been identified: built-up area is 18.14%, agriculture land 49.45%, Water Bodies 9.67%, Scrub 
Land 2.94%, River 9.24%, open forest 0.24% and prawn ponds 9.88%. 
 
Seismicity: The proposed project corridors fall in Zone III as per revised seismic zoning map 
of India.  
 
0.4.2  Water Environment  
 
Water Resources  of the District : The western and north western parts of the dis trict is 
covered by Alluvium. This aquifer can broadly be demarcated into two zones namely newer 
alluvium and older alluvium. As per the CGWB report of Surat district, the depths of the 

[page 17]
EIA and SIA Study for Surat Metro Rail Project Phase-I 
EIA Report December 2020 4 
 
wells in alluvium genera lly range from 3 to 30 m with some of the well s having bores down 
to 50m below bottom of the wells. The depth to water level in Surat city varies from 10m to 
20m but at some places it goes up up to 5m.  
 
Flood Prone Areas: Surat lies in the flood plain and  also lies in an estuarial region. There are 
number of creeks in the south - western part of the city. The city has been historically 
susceptible to flooding. Development of Surat in the past has been affected due to the high 
flood in Tapi  however appropria te measures have been taken  like construction of 
embankment, river training and river front development etc.  
 
Drainage: The Tapi is the major river which passes through the central parts of the district 
and flows towards the west. The walled city area has no good drainage system. There are 
few pockets of low lying area, where water gets logged in the rainy season. It is observe d 
that the area of Navasari Bazar, Khapatia Chakla, Panini Bhit, Vada Chauhta area get flooded 
in rainy season.  
 
Water Quality:  The water sampling was done on the basis of avai lability of ground water 
and surface water source along the proposed metro alig nments. There were no water in the 
canal during the field study (May 19). Hence 10 surface water samples from small water 
ponds were collected and other 4 samples were collected  from ground water source. 
Parameters like pH, calcium, floride and ntrate are found within permissible limits in both 
surface and ground water samples. Hard water found in 3 locatons, TDS is very hign i.e. 
18716 mg/l in Tapi river due to salt water intrus ion in high tide from creek. Also It is found 
high in 2 other locations. High total cound to phytoplanktones found in creek water and one 
of canal water due to coastal water pushing in more phytoplankton population towards 
mouth of the estuary. Zooplankton  standing stock was studied in terms of biomass, 
population. Highest count was found in creek water sample.  
 
0.4.3  Ambient Environment  
 
Climate: Meteorological data like monthly total rainfall, maximum & minimum temperature 
and relative humidity of the Surat for the period of Jan 2014 to Dec 2018  have been collected 
from IMD. Monthly wind rose diagram for last 10 years i.e. 2009 to 2018 at 8:30 hrs and 
17:30 hrs have also been collected from IMD. T he predominant direction is from NW and 
SW for 08:30 hrs and N, NW and SW direction for 17:30 hrs. 
 
Ambient Air Quality:  20 ambient air monitoring stations were selected strategically to 
generate the representative samples for ambient air quality covering residential, 
institutional and industrial area along the corridors. Two days continuous air quality 
monitoring at 20 locations along the metro corridors has been done in the month of May – 
June 2019. Five major air pollutants viz. particulate matter (PM10 & PM2.5), Sulphur Dioxide 
(SO2), Nitrogen Dioxide (NO2) a nd Carbon Monoxide (CO) representing the ai r quality were 
monitored. The air quality monitoring results indicate that all parameters are within the 
permissible limits of National Standards . When these results compared with IFC standards, 
PM10 is exceeding the permissible limits at all locations and PM 2.5 exceeding at few 
locations. 
 

[page 18]
EIA and SIA Study for Surat Metro Rail Project Phase-I 
EIA Report December 2020 5 
 
Noise Environment:  Noise level survey was carried out at 20 locations along the metro 
corridor. The noise levels were collected over 24 hours in the month of May - June 2019 at 
hourly interval during morning, afternoon and evening such that peak and off peak hours 
are covered.  Noise Monitoring results shows that noise level at all locations is exceeding 
the noise level standards prescribed by CPCB either day or nig ht or both for  Residential 
Zone, Commercial Zone; and when compared with IFC standards within the permissible 
limits at few locations and at remaning locations exceeding the permissible limits.  The main 
source of noise in the project area is the traffic movement on the road.  
 
Vibration: Vibration monitoring was carried out at 10 locations for 24 hrs continuous along 
the corridor in the month of May & June 2019. Vibration monitoring was carried out using 
the Nomis Seismograph equipment which can measure the radial, trans verse and vertical  
vibration of ground-borne vibrations.  
 
0.4.4 Ecological Environment  
 
Trees survey was carried out in the month of May - June 2019 along the proposed metro 
alignments and at station locations. About 2093 trees observed along both the corridors and 
depot. The height and girth of the trees ranges from 3m to 14m and 30cm to 400cm 
respectively. Two giant African Baobab ( Adansonia digitata) trees are observed within ROW 
opposite Croma Shopping Complex & Jodiyar Mata Mandir near proposed Adaja n Gam -
station alon g Bhesan to Saroli corridor. Out of these two Giant biobab tree, one tree is 
collapsed due to weather disturbance on 02 -05-2020. Surat Municipal Corporation 
proposed to preserve the felled tree at the same place.   
 
Mangrove: The Avicennia marina  is the predominant mangrove species found at the left 
bank of Tapi river. Four mangrove shrubs of about 3m height and 30cm girth are found at 
the left bank of river Tapi near Chowpati Garden (Jawaharlal Nehru Park). 
 
0.4.5  Sensitive Receptors 
 
Sensitive receptors such as educational institutes (schools, colleges, universities etc), 
hospitals, and religious places within 100 m from the central line of the proposed metro rail 
corridor has been demarcated. In the field study about 188 sensitive recep tors found within 
100 m from the centre line of proposed metro corridors. 
 
0.5 Negative Environmental Impacts 
  
0.5.1 Impacts due to Project Location  
 
Change of Land use:  Land will be required permanently for stations, and running sections. 
Both government and private land will be acquired for the project and the details are given 
in civil engineering in the DPR. No forest land is required for the proposed project corridors. 
 
Impact on Ecological Environment:  Total about 2093 number of trees are likely to be 
affected due to the proposed project, out of which about 932 trees to be cut and 1161 trees 
are to be transplant . No rare endangered endemic and threatened (REET) species are 
observed during the field study.  

[page 19]
EIA and SIA Study for Surat Metro Rail Project Phase-I 
EIA Report December 2020 6 
 
Impact on Archaeological Monuments/Heritage : No Archaeological and Heritage are 
directly affected due to the proposed metro rail project.  
 
0.5.2 Impacts due to Project Design  
 
Right of Way, Alignment and Architecture:  In case of underground metro the space at 
ground level and above can continue t o be used for roads and light structures and visual 
intrusion will be minimize.  
 
Inter Model Integraion:  Physical and operational integration of metro with other modes 
especially walk, public transport and intermediate public transport (hired modes) is found to 
increase ridership and decrease congestion inside and outside the stations. 
 
Use of Energy and Water at stations and depots:  Consumption of energy is significantly 
reduced by proper design of open spa ces at stations and multimodal integration facili ties 
outside stations. Water required at stations and Depot for non -drinking purposes like 
horticulture and toilets can be sourced from recycled water.  
 
0.5.3 Impacts due to Project Construction  
 
Air pollu tion: Particulate pollution occurs due to excavat ion, loading and unloading of 
construction material, vehicular movement, construction equipment and DG sets etc. It also 
occurs in sites of muck disposal, debris disposal and pre -casting yards. Resulting pol lution is 
short term.  
 
Noise Pollution: Noise generated from construction equipment (Lmax) in the range of 94 -
124 dB(A), which decreases with increase in distance. The noise levels due to different 
activities during construction phase will be reduced to a mbient noise levels at a distance of 
125 m. These  estimated noise levels are typical and will vary with specifications of the 
equipment, usage pattern etc.  
 
Vibration Impacts and Risk to Existing Buildings: Threshold criteria for various structures 
are given in the report. Vibration source levels for ty pical construction equipment are also 
listed in the report. Vibration threshold criteria is predicted for all the sensitive receptors.  
 
Impact due to Muck Disposal: Construction activities will result in excavation of about 6.89 
lakh cum and it will be us ed in backfilling of the piles and rest will be disposed off properly. 
t. Muck disposal if not properly done can result in air and water pollution, noise, diversion of 
green parks and temporary displacement. Site selection criteria for muck is proposed in the 
Chapter 7. 
 
Impact due to Hazardous Waste:  Hazardous waste would mainly arise from the 
maintenance of equipment. Unsafe disposal can result in water and soil pollution.  
 
Increased Water Demand: The water demand is estimated to be 670 kilolitres per day (KLD) 
for construction activities.  
 

[page 20]
EIA and SIA Study for Surat Metro Rail Project Phase-I 
EIA Report December 2020 7 
 
Impact on Water Quality:  Ground water contamination can take place if sewage at labour 
camps or chemical substances from constr uction site or dumped muck or 
construction/demolition waste or used water from the RMC pla nt get leached by 
precipitation of water and percolate to the ground water table.  
 
Soil Erosion and land subsidence:  Run off from unprotected excavated areas can res ult in 
excessive soil erosion.  
 
Traffic Diversions: Complete/partial traffic diversions o n road will be required, as most of 
the construction activities are on the road.  
 
Impacts due to Labour Camps: Improper disposal of solid waste generated by labour camps 
can pollute surface water bodies and groundwate r. The water requirement at labour cam p 
is the responsibility of the contractor. Construction workers are more prone to infectious 
diseases due to unsafe sexual activity and lack of sanitation facilities and insect vectors. 
Problems could arise due to cult ural differences between workers from outside and local 
residents.  
 
Impact due to Supply of Construction Material:  Poor choice of source and quarrying 
operations cause dust pollution and wastage of natural resources.  
 
0.5.4 Impacts due to Project Operation  
 
Noise Pollution: Effect of predicted day-time noise level has low impact with respect to the 
existing ambient noise environment. The predicted noise levels at all sensitive r eceptors 
along the corridor were predicted. Noise mitigation measures proposed to mitigate 
operational noise of metro.  
 
Vibration: Passing of trains on elevated section causes vibrations. The dominant component 
of vibration due to passing on elevated section is horizontal. The resultant magnitudes of 
PPV at all sensitive receptors are predicted and are within permissible limits. 
 
Energy Consumption at Stations:  Energy is required at stations for facilities like lighting, 
passenger information, access, security, climate control, escalators/elevators etc. 
 
Water Supply, Waste Water and municipal solid waste disposal at S tations: The total 
water demand, waste water gnereated and solid waste generation has been estimated for 
the horizon years 2021, 2026, 2036 and 2046. It is assumed that 15% of Alighting passengers 
at each station will use the toilets. The water demand duri ng operation phase will be 
supplied by Municipal Corporation.  
 
0.5.5 Impacts due to Depot  
 
Water supply: It will be required for different purposes in the depot. The water demand at 
both the depots would be about 259 KLD for train washing and 46 KLD for domestic purpose 
for official staffs and staff quarters. Water requirement for other purposes like horticu lture, 
flushing urinals/closet will be met from recycled water. 
 

[page 21]

EIA Report December 2020 8 
 
Effluent/Wastewater Generation: About 210 KLD of waste water will be generated from 
washing of trains and 41 KLD sewage will be generated at both the depots.   
 
Oil Pollution: Oil spillage du ring change of lubricants, cleaning and repair processes, in the 
maintenance Depot cum workshop for maintenance of rolling stock, is very common. The 
spilled oil should be trapped in oil and grease trap. 
 
Noise Pollution: The main source of noise from depot is the operation of workshop. 
  
Loss of Trees:  About 319 number of trees are observed at Bhesan depot site  and 176 trees 
are to be removed at Dream City depot. Out of this 453 trees will be cut and 42 trees will be 
transplanted. 
 
0.6 Positive Environmental Impacts  
 
Employment Opportunities:  In operation phase, about 2856 people will be employed for 
operation and maintenance of the system. Thus, the p roject would provide substantial 
direct employment and consequent indirect employment.  
 
Benefits to Economy:  These corridors will yield benefits in terms of growth in economi c 
activity due to better accessibility, savings in fuel consumption, correspondin g reduction in 
cost of road construction and maintenance, reduction in vehicle operating costs, savings in 
travel time, improvement in quality of life and reduction in loss of productivity due to health 
disorders resulting from pollution and accidents.  
 
Direct Benefits to Passengers: The project will result in direct benefits to users of Metro like 
reduction in vehicle operating costs, savings in travel time, improvement in qu ality of life, 
reduction in loss of productivity due to health disorders resultin g from pollution and 
reduction in road accidents.  
 
Traffic Noise Reduction:  Reduction in traffic volume of 10% & 50% reduces noise at the 
tune of 0.5 dB & 3.0 dB respectively.  
 
Reduction of Traffic on Road: The basis of reduction of vehicle is shift of ridership from road 
vehicle to the metro railway. The reduction in number of vehicles gives benefits to economy 
by reduction in Vehicle Operating Cost (VOC), Fuel Consumption, Pollution Load, Accidents 
and Travel Time etc.  
 
Reduced Air Pollution: The operation of metro will reduce generation of CO, PM, HC & NOx 
and CO 2; which will reduce the health impacts along with reduction in cost of carbon 
capture.  
 
Carbon Credits: CO2 emissions due Energy consumption (Traction and Auxiliary) and 
reduction due to rege neration energy for year 20 21 are estimated based on the CO 2 
Intensity of Electricity from grid as given in CMP Tool Kit 2014.

0.7 Environment Management Plan  
 
Compensatory Afforestation: It is proposed to plant three saplings for each tree to be cut 
and one sapling for each tree to be transplant; hence 3957 trees need to be planted. The 
native plant species and miscellaneous indigenous tree species are recommended for 
afforestation. Forest Department has identified 15 ha of Forest land near village An aval for 
Afforestation.  
 
Right of Way, Alignment and Architecture: Viaduct and elevated stations shall be shaped to 
minimize visual intrusion.  
 
Spatial Planning of Stations and Inter-Modal Integration: Adequate and well-laid out space 
shall be designed f or concourses and platforms, escalators, elevators and staircases, 
lighting, turnstiles for normal and abnormal operating conditions; optimal height / depth of 
the stations, force d ventilation shall be provided. Physical and operational integration of 
metro with other modes shall be planned.  
 
Green Buildings:  Green building  (also known as  sustainable building) refers to both a 
structure and the using of processes that are environm entally responsible and resource -
efficient throughout a  building's life-cycle: from siting to design, construction, operation, 
maintenance, renovation, and demolition. Green buildings help in better preservation of 
environment as in such structures there a re provisions for better saving of energy, water 
and CO2. Such buildings also have better waste management arrangements. All stations and 
Depot buildings can be designed as green buildings. 
 
Solar Power:  For the utilization of renewable energy, wherever fe asible, installations for 
solar power can be implemented on the roof of eleva ted stations and in Depots. It is 
assumed that 75% of roof area of each station is considered for solar power generation. 
Solar energy generation per year is estimated to be 3.45 Giga-watt-hr for Sarthana to Dream 
City corridor and 4.44 Giga-watt-hr for Bhesan to Saroli Corridor. 
 
Use of Energy and Water:  Requirement of electrical energy for climate control, lighting and 
other facilities at stations shall be optimized by proper use of day light and design of 
passenger flow inside stations and on streets out side stations. To augment the water 
requirement Rain Water Harvesting is proposed at elevated station and alignment.  
 
Utility Plan: Prior to the execution of work at site, detailed investigation of all utilities will be 
undertaken and plans for their rete ntion in situ with precautions or temporary/perman ent 
diversions prepared and got approved by respective agencies  
 
Construction  and Demoliation Waste  Management: The procurement source of the 
construction materials will be decided by the Contractor, but it will be from the licensed 
supplier. Sites for c asting of structural concrete elements and material stockpiling will be 
decided before start of construction.  
 
Muck Disposal: The excavated soil generated from the construction of proposed metro will 
be utilized for backfilling of piles and remaining will  be disposed off environmental friendly 
manner.  

[page 23]
EIA and SIA Study for Surat Metro Rail Project Phase-I 
EIA Report December 2020 10 
 
Energy Management:  The contractor shall use and maintain equipment and employ 
recommended practices so as to conserve energy.  
 
Labour Camp, welfare and Safe ty: The facilities to be provided by the Contracto r are water 
supply, waste water and sewage treatment; Solid Waste Management; Health care 
awareness and clinics; Shelter at Workplace, Canteen Facilities, and First aid facilities. 
Construction works shall be executed as laid down in the Safety Health and Environment 
(SH&E) Manual prepared by the Contractor and approved by the Implementing Agency.  
 
Hazardous Waste Management: It shall be stored and disposed of by the Contractor as per 
Hazardous and Other Wa stes (Management, Handling & Trans -boundary movement) Rules 
2019.  
 
Air Pollution and Noise Pollution Control: Mitigation measures as per SH&E manual shall be 
adopted during the construction period.  
 
Vibration Control:  Construction activities shall be sch eduled such that demolition, 
earthmoving and groun d-impacting operations do not occur in the same time period. At 
locations where the alignment is close to sensitive structures, the contractor shall prepare a 
monitoring scheme prior to construction at such  locations. In case of sensitive structures, 
vibration mitigation measures will be implemented.  
 
Water pollution Control: Wash water shall be led through separate drains into precipitation 
chambers before discharging into the sewage drain to the standards prescribed for disposal.  
Traffic Diversion/Manag ement: Measures such as road widening, traffic segregation, one -
way movements, traffic diversions, acquisition of service lanes, etc. will be employed.  
 
Soil Erosion and Land Subsidnece Control: Excavation shall be limited; temporary berms 
and use of temporary mulches, fabrics, mats, seeding, or other control devices or methods 
shall be implemented.  
 
Noise Management:  Noise barriers  are proposed wherever required to reduce the noise 
impact. 
  
Water Supply and Sanitation at Stations: Water supply for drinking, washing of stations, air 
conditioning and other uses will be procured from municipal authorities. This will be 
supplemented by  re-use of treated waste water generated by staff and passengers. The 
waste water will be treated by installing bio digesters at each station. Non -hazardous solid 
waste generated at stations will be collected, segregated and disposed as per Solid Waste 
Management Rules 2016.  
 
Rain Water Harvesting:  To conserve and augment ground water, it has been proposed to 
construct rainwater harvesting structure of suita ble capacity along the viaduct and at depot 
sites. 
 
Disaster Risk Management: The recommended approac h (UNISDR) is to manage disaster 
risk rather than managing disasters. Disas ter risk is the combination of the severity and 

[page 24]
EIA and SIA Study for Surat Metro Rail Project Phase-I 
EIA Report December 2020 11 
 
frequency of a hazard, the numbers of people and assets exposed to the hazard, and their 
vulnerability to damage. 
 
Training: The training for engineers and managers will be imparted by GMRCL on regular 
basis to implement the environmental protection clauses of the tender document and to 
implement the best environmental practices during the construction phase and ensure 
preparedness for disaster prevention.  
 
By implementing the mitigation measures and management plans detailed in Environmental 
Managmenet Plan, the project is expected to comply with the Indian Standards and WB ESF 
guidelines. 
 
0.8 Public Consultations  
 
Project l evel public consultations consultations were conducted at various locations along 
the alignments during first week of November 2019. Public consultations were conducted at 
10 locations where p eople from different communities were participated. During public 
consultation, issues or suggestion raised by the stakeholders related to tree removal, air and 
noise pollution, comfort and safety, traffic congestion, employment generations etc.  
 
0.9 Environmental Monitoring Plan  
 
Environmental monitoring will be carried out for air quality, noise levels, vibration, water 
quality and ecology during construction and operation phases of the project. The monitoring 
report of environmental parameters (Air, No ise, Water and soil) will be prepared by the 
environmental engineer and submitted to the Project Management Consultant. Project 
diaries would record  environmental problems (soil erosion, air quality, water quality, noise 
level etc), as well as safety incidents and will be retained.  
 
0.9.1 Establishment of Environment Division  
 
It is recommended that establishment of an Environment Division at the in itial stage of the 
project itself with an Environmental Officer and an Environment Engineer. The task of the  
division would be to supervise and coordinate studies, environmental monitoring and 
implementation of environmental mitigation measures, and it should report directly to Chief 
Engineer of the Project Authority.  
 
0.10 Cost Estimates  
 
The total estimated environmental management and monitoring cost for the proposed 
project is Rs 3656.44 lakh.

Chapter 1 : Introduction 
1.1. Background 
Surat, a city in the Indian state of Gujarat is the eighth largest city and ninth largest urban 
agglomeration in India. The city with its centre on the banks of river Tapti, is located 284 km 
south of the state capital, Gandhinagar; and 289 km north of Mumbai. The city Surat was 
the world's 4th fastest growing city in 2016 according to a study conducted by the City 
Mayors Foundation and has been selected as one of twenty Indian cities to be developed as 
a smart city under PM Narendra Modi's flagship Smart Cities Mission. Famous for it s 
diamond cutting and polishing is known as the Diamond City of India. Traditionally, th e 
major industries in the city were textile manufacturing and shipbuilding. The British 
established their first Indian factory (trading post) at Surat in 1612. The city  gradually 
declined throughout the 18th century. However, by the mid -19th century Surat was the 
fastest growing city of the Gujarat state with a population of more than 5.5 million with an 
area of 326.515 sq km.  
Recognizing the need to cater to ever incre asing population of the city and provide 
commuters an environment friendly , fast and reliable travel option, present Government of 
Gujarat decided to take steps to implement metro rail transit systems (MRTS) in cities 
having population more than 2 Million.  As a part of this decision, Metro Rail Project for 
Surat is being initiat ed as the city population is more than 5.5 million. Surat Municipal 
Corporation in association with Gujarat Urban Development Company (GUDC) Ltd., 
Gandhinagar is exploring the feasib ility of the project. The detailed project report (DPR) for 
Surat Metro Rail was prepared by Delhi Metro Rail Corporation (DMRC) in June 2018. 
In August 2012, Surat Municipal Corporation (SMC) proposed four routes for the Metro 
project. The Government of Gujarat approved two corridors in January 2017. In March 2019, 
Union Government approved the Surat Metro Rail Project with two metro rail corridors and 
with a combined length of 40.35 km. The first corridor is proposed from Sarthana to Dream 
City Line cover ing a distance of 21.61 km. The second corridor is proposed from Bhesan to  
Saroli covering a distance of 18.74 km. These two corridors will intersect at Majura Gate.  
Gujarat Metro Rail Corporation (GMRC) Ltd. has further awarded the consultancy work to 
RITES Limited for preparation of Environmental Impact Assessment and Social Impact 
Assessment study reports vide LOA No. GMRC/EIA & SIA/SURAT/PH -1/ 2019 Dated 20 th 
March, 2019. The assignment will cover the Surat Metro Rail project’s two corridors, i.e. 
from Bhesan to Saroli and Sarthana to Dream city along with two depot sites. 
 
 
 

[page 26]
EIA and SIA Study for Surat Metro Rail Project Phase-I 
EIA Report December 2020 13 
 
1.2. Project Area 
The proposed metro rail system will pass through Sarthana to Dream City Line and from 
Bhesan to Saroli of Surat, Gujarat. The coordinates for Surat are 21°15:21°8' N a nd 
72°44':72°53' E with a total geographical area of about 327 sq. km. The city is situat ed in the 
southern part of the state and is bounded by the Arabian Sea on the west, Bharuch in the 
North, the Valsad district in the south, the Dangs district in the s outh-east, and the Tapi 
district in the east. The Surat district has been divided into ni ne talukas namely Bardoli, 
Choryasi, Kamrej, Mahuva, Mandvi, Mangrol, Olpad, Palsana and Umarpada. Geographically, 
the district belongs to the western coastlands of th e Deccan peninsula. Major geological 
formations exposed in the district are Quaternary al luvium, Tertiary limestone and 
sandstones and Deccan Trap basalt. The Tapi is the major river which passes through the 
central parts of the district also throughout Surat city and flows towards the west. In the last 
50 years, maximum temperature recorded in Surat ranged from 30.6 0C to 37.7 0C however 
the average temperature of Surat is 27.2 0C. Surat's climate is classified as tropical. The 
summers here have a good dea l of rainfall, while the winters have very little. April and May 
are the hottest months. With the onset of monsoons, temperature decreases appreciably in 
June but remains steady thereafter till September. The average annual rainfall is 1192 mm.  
 
As per provisional reports of Census of India, population of Surat in 2011 was 4,467,797; of 
which male and female are 2,543,623 and 1,924,174 respectively. Population of Surat 
metropolitan was 4,591,246 of which 2,619,373 are males and 1,971,873 are females. The 
literacy rate of Sur at is higher than the national average at 88%. Male literacy has been 
recorded at 91.22%, while it is 83.44% for females. There are more men than women in the 
city. There is also a high rate of young people, with 12% of the total population being under 
the age of 6. The official language of the city is Gujarati. The majority of people in Surat  
associate themselves with Hinduism, minor religions including Islam, Jainism and 
Christianity.  
Industrial development in Surat district is attributed to the presence of a large number of 
diamond processing, textiles and chemical & petrochemical industries. 
1.3. Objective of the Study 
The objective of the study is to facilitate the Gujarat Metro Rail Cor poration (GMRC) Ltd. in 
preparation of EIA report as per the requirement of regulatory and  funding agency. The 
objective of the study as per the Terms of Reference is as follows: 
• To collect all possible primary and secondary data on environmental baseline 
information, that may affect environmental components of the aligned area; 
• To assess and calculate potential impacts on natural environment and impacts 
caused by the proposed alignment. 

[page 27]
EIA and SIA Study for Surat Metro Rail Project Phase-I 
EIA Report December 2020 14 
 
• To prepare Environmental Management and Monitoring Plan (EMMP) for the 
necessary actions to minimise potential environmental impacts as well as to propose 
proper mitigation measures. 
• To disseminate the information of Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) studies. 
As per LOA, EIA study shall cover the entire requirement un der the environmental related 
legislation in India, JICA Guidelines 2010 for environmental and social considerations and 
World Bank Operational Policy (OP) 4.12. As infor med by GMRCL, the project is funded by 
AFD & KfW, accordingly the report has been prep ared as per requirement of world bank ESF 
2017 and performance standards of IFC.  
1.4. Scope of EIA Study 
The scope of work for the EIA study as given in the Letter  of Award vide letter GMRC/EIA & 
SIA/SURAT/PH-1/2019 dated March 20, 2019 is as follows: 
 
The EIA  study will include the environmental management plan (EMP) and environmental 
monitoring plan. The proposed EIA study shall cover following in detail. 
 
• Description of the Proposed Project (Provide a brief description of the relevant parts of 
the project, using maps (at appropriate scale) where necessary, and including the 
following information: location; general layout; size, capacity, etc.; pre -construction 
activities; co nstruction activities; schedule; facilities and services; operation and 
maintenance activies; required offsite investments; and life span) 
• Legislative and Regulatory Considerations (Describe the pertinent regulations and 
standards governing environmental q uality, health and safety, protection of sensitive 
areas, protection of enda ngered species, sitting, land use control, etc., at international, 
national, regional and local levels) 
• Description of the Environment. 
(a) Physical environment: geology; topography; soils; climate and meteorology; ambient 
air quality; surface and ground - water hydrology; coastal and oceanic parameters; 
existing sources of air emissions; existing water pollution discharges; and receiving 
water quality. 
(b) Biological environment: flora; fau na; rare or endangered species; sensitive habitats, 
including parks or prese rves, significant natural sites, etc.; species of commercial 
importance; and species with potential to become nuisances, vectors or dangerous. 
• Identification of the boundary of recorded forests / mangroves; 
• Collection of information from / and interview wi th forest department, academicians 
and local NGOs; 
• Estimate the number of trees to be cut (Height, diameter, species etc.); 
• Field reconnaissance: Vegetation distribution, important fauna and endangered fauna; 

[page 28]
EIA and SIA Study for Surat Metro Rail Project Phase-I 
EIA Report December 2020 15 
 
• Inventory Survey of Sensitive Receptor; 
• Noise & Vibration measurement; 
• Identification of major rivers/ creeks likely to be affected, measurement of water 
quality in the identified rivers/creeks and collection of existing and pu blished data on 
water quality; 
• Existing data on air quality, water quality, soil, status on solid waste, etc. will be 
collected, interviews with the experts on the relevant field will be conducted, if 
necessary; 
• Description of the alternatives extends to s iting, design, technology selection, 
construction techniques and phasing, an d operating and maintenance procedures. 
Compare alternatives in terms of potential environmental impacts, capital and 
operating costs, suitability under local conditions, and insti tutional, training, and 
monitoring requirements. This alternative analysis w ill be done subject to the 
availability of alternative alignments from GMRCL. 
• Determination of the potential impacts due to proposed project (negative, positive, 
direct and indirect) during the project cycle. 
• Preparation of Environment Management Plan (EMP) and Environment Monitoring Plan 
(EMoP) based on analysis of collected data, mitigation strategy, EMoP will be finalised 
incorporating feedback from local residents participated i n Public Consultation 
Meetings and ensuring good environmental quality through monitoring; 
• Identification of Institutional needs to Implement Environmental Assessment 
Recommendations (Review the authority and capability of institutions and recommend 
steps to strengthen or expand them so that the management and monitoring plans in 
the environmental assessment can be implemented); 
• Assist in coordinating the environmental assessment with other government agencies, 
in obtaining the views of local NGO’s and affected groups,  
• Explore opportunities for claiming carbon credits against this  project including 
methodologies and estimated value of credits. 
1.5. Approach and Methodology  
The Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) report will be conducted as per the ToR issued 
by the GMRC Limited and EIA & EMP guidelines of World bank. The impact zone for the 
trees for this report comprises area under Right of Way (RoW) and working space of the 
proposed Metro line, Stations, Depots and permanent ancillary facilities if any. Howe ver the 
study area comprises green area, river and environmental sensitive areas around the project 
site. So that, secondary data has been collected within 15km radius of proposed alignment 
and inventory of environmental sensitive features has been carried  out within 100m on 
either side of centre line of alignment. The method ology is highlighted in the following 
paragraphs. 
 

[page 29]
EIA and SIA Study for Surat Metro Rail Project Phase-I 
EIA Report December 2020 16 
 
The study should ascertain the existing baseline conditions and assess the impacts as a 
result of construction and operation of the pr oject. The changes likely to occur in different 
components of the envir onment viz. physical, biological and socio -economic etc. shall be 
studied, analyzed and quantified, wherever possible. The accurate analysis of assessment 
depends upon the reliable data  generated/available on environment. The consultant has 
documented the baseline data for various parameters of ecology (Flora & Fauna), 
environmental pollution (air, water, noise, vibration and solid waste) and socio -economic 
(public health, education and economics).  
The standard methodology for the data collection, impact a ssessment and formulation of 
management plans is adopted. The National Acts, Legislation and Laws along with World 
bank are consulted with a view to ensure compliance with various requirements. 
 
Baseline Data Collection: The existing land -use pattern of the area has been identified 
mainly as urban human settlements, roads, Trees and water bodies. Physical and chemical 
parameters of soils along the project corridor are studied from the sa mples collected and 
analysed. Based on this data, impact on soil has been predicted.  
The water samples are collected from surface water sources and ground water source (bore 
wells) for analysis as per IS: 10500 -2012. The secondary data, if any, has been s tudied and 
compiled. The impact on w ater quality due to the proposed project activities has been 
evaluated.  
Air and Noise  quality is an important consideration during construction and operation 
phases. Ambient air quality and noise levels are monitored in  and around project area to 
develop present baseline levels in the area. Location of Air & Noise monitoring have been 
identified on the basis of landuse of the area.  
Vibration levels are measured along the project corridor along the sensitive identified 
locations with the objective to estab lish the baseline data and assess the impacts of 
vibration at important receptors. There is no sensitive receptors i.e. heritage structures etc. 
Terrestrial ecology was studied along the proposed metro corridors. The veg etation types 
documented through the  visual inspection, past research and field investigations. A survey 
was carried out in the project area to find the existing flora and fauna. The list of birds, 
animals, aquatic ecology etc. of the area is compiled alon g with the existence of any rare 
and endangered species. 
Meteorological data: The monthly temperature, relative humidity, rainfall, Wind rose and 
cloud cover of meteorological stations at Surat was collected from the Indian 
Meteorological Department, Ahmedabad. 
Based on project particulars a nd the existing environmental conditions, potential impacts 
are identified that are expected to be affected as a result of the proposed project and 

[page 30]
EIA and SIA Study for Surat Metro Rail Project Phase-I 
EIA Report December 2020 17 
 
wherever possible, these are quantified. Both positive and negative impa cts are evaluated 
to have an idea ab out resultant impacts. These impacts are assessed for various phases of 
project cycle namely, location & design, construction and operation. The standard 
methodology is adopted for impact prediction and assessment.  
An e nvironmental management strategy is developed to mitigate the adverse impacts 
during construction and operation phases of the project. The strategy includes evaluation of 
alternative methods to reduce or eliminate adverse impacts of the most critical areas  likely 
to contribute to the most si gnificant environmental burdens. The Environmental 
Management Plan (EMP) would highlight the proposed mitigation measures to be 
implemented during project construction phase like compensatory afforestation plan, 
infrastructure facilities like sanitation, labour camps, and refuse disposal etc. Cost estimates 
for each of the proposed mitigation measures are given.  
Consultation and communication with stakeholders during the project preparation is an 
integral part of the process of gathering relevant data for impact assessment, and facilitates 
the development of appropriate options for the affected population.  
It is necessary to monitor the environmental attributes during construction and operation. 
Monitoring indicates any environmental problems. This will fa cilitate to assess the 
effectiveness of management / mitigation measures. The post project environmental 
monitoring program is designed for implementation along with the cost. The Budget 
estimates and equipments necessary for the effective implementation o f the Environmental 
Management Plan and environmental monitoring are worked out. The approach for the 
study is presented in Figure 1.1. 
 
For the preparation of this EIA, the project team and environmental experts have li aised 
with the GMRC Limited in order  to discuss the proposed scope of the EIA, available data in 
the specific area on environmental attributes and general comments / observations that 
these authorities may have on the project and its environs.

2.1. Environmental Policy Framework 
 
The environmental policy framework include existing institutions and legislations relevant to 
the project at the International, National and State levels. The various statutory clearances/ 
permissions from state and central government authorities and inst itutional framework are 
discussed in the subsequent section.  
 
2.2. Legal Framework 
 
The proposed project would be governed by various Acts, Rules and regulations set by the 
Ministry of Environment, Forests and Climate Change (MoEFCC) at the Central level and 
other regulatory agencies at the State and local level. Various environmental standards, 
specifications and guidelines of Central Pollution Control Board (CPCB) and state level 
agencies will also be applicable. 
 
It is important to mention over here that th e Central government framed an ‘umbrella law’, 
called the Environment (Protection) Act, 1986  to broadly encompass and regulate an array 
of environmental issues. The overall purpose of EPA was to establish an overall coherent 
policy and provide a basis for the coordinated work of various government agencies with 
operational responsibility for the environment and natural resources. The legislation also 
invests authorities with regulatory powers to address specific issues affecting the 
environment. The Act als o does not allow any person to carry on an industry, operation or 
process that discharge or emit any environmental pollutants in excess of standards 
prescribed under specific rules and notifications. 
 
The Acts, Rules and Notifications applicable to environ mental aspects of the constructional 
and operational phases of the proposed project are summarized and briefly described in the 
Table 2.1 below.
